## Leadership

### Fw: SWLW #715: The Antithesis Principle, Don't be a Meat Proxy, and more.
_uloza+hey@proton.me_

- This week's featured pick, 'The Antithesis Principle,' argues wise people apply hard truths about human nature to themselves before using them as tactics on others
- Multiple articles examine AI's real impact on labor and coding: agents trained on open collaborative artifacts (specs, PRs, reviews) outperform imitation-only training, and rapid AI-driven shipping isn't necessarily boosting customer demand or utilization
- Other highlights include advice against copy-pasting AI output verbatim ('Don't Be a Meat Proxy'), skepticism around inconsistent ARR metrics in VC pitches, and data/culture lessons from Meta on schema enforcement and shrinking management layers

> [The Antithesis Principle](https://us.list-manage.com/1AWH8Q19U9h?e=86169b0ba2&c2id=4541cead0ded4f764194eec0f1f5b20f)
> 5 minutes read.
>
> Shreyas Doshi is spot on. Any truth about human nature can be pointed two ways: outward as a tactic for working people, or inward as a warning about your own defaults. Smart people chase the tactic, wise people eliminate the default in themselves first: "You will not only reach the obvious conclusion that everyone who’s smart reaches, but perhaps also the non-obvious one that only the wise do." Which one are you actually doing right now?

> [Don't Be a Meat Proxy](https://us.list-manage.com/uQoESsEEXAq?e=86169b0ba2&c2id=4541cead0ded4f764194eec0f1f5b20f)
> 2 minutes read.
>
> Verbose writing is often lazy writing. Copying it straight from the output you got from AI is an even worse type of laziness. Niklas Gruhn's sharpest reframe: writing a response in your own words isn't about politeness, it's a decent certificate that you actually read. Think what Hemingway would write; make it a skill if you have to.
>
> Readit later via[Instapaper](https://us.list-manage.com/Yatd8RMXsok?e=86169b0ba2&c2id=4541cead0ded4f764194eec0f1f5b20f).
> Shareit via[Twitter](https://us.list-manage.com/mwEPsfh2P57?e=86169b0ba2&c2id=4541cead0ded4f764194eec0f1f5b20f)or[email](mailto:?subject=don%27t+be+a+meat+proxy&body=Great post: https://gruhn.me/blog/2026-08-03/%0d%0a%0d%0a%0d%0aFound it via http://softwareleadweekly.com/).
>
> [What's Gone Wrong With AI & Labor — A Thought Experiment](https://us.list-manage.com/bpFODug023S?e=86169b0ba2&c2id=4541cead0ded4f764194eec0f1f5b20f)
> 4 minutes read.
>
> Arvind Narayanan reaches for Simon Willison's line to explain why coding agents haven't replaced engineers: "Quitting programming as a career right now because of LLMs would be like quitting carpentry as a career thanks to the invention of the table saw." -- Our industry open-sourced every intermediate artifact, from specs to pull requests to code reviews, giving agents a training set that teaches collaboration instead of just imitation. Every profession chasing its own AI moment should be asking whether it can manufacture that same missing middle, or whether it's stuck training AI on finished outputs alone.
>
> Readit later via[Instapaper](https://us.list-manage.com/j9njItS6jDC?e=86169b0ba2&c2id=4541cead0ded4f764194eec0f1f5b20f).
> Shareit via[Twitter](https://us.list-manage.com/EYXSRLezQ4H?e=86169b0ba2&c2id=4541cead0ded4f764194eec0f1f5b20f)or[email](mailto:?subject=what%27s+gone+wrong+with+ai+%26+labor+%e2%80%94+a+thought+experiment&body=Great post: https://x.com/random_walker/status/2082163285588107752%0d%0a%0d%0a%0d%0aFound it via http://softwareleadweekly.com/).
>
> [ARR Doesn't Mean What It Used To](https://us.list-manage.com/1AtL1iQ8Lae?e=86169b0ba2&c2id=4541cead0ded4f764194eec0f1f5b20f)
> 5 minutes read.
>
> "ARR... Is it revenue or GMV? Is it recurring? Is it quarterly \* 4? Or is it 365 \* daily revenue (not kidding - have seen that too!!)?" -- Niko Bonatsos sums up why Simon Wu's poll of VCs found ARR itself the most distrusted metric in the room right now. Which flavor of ARR is sitting in your own deck? Founders who spell out their ARR math up front build more credibility than any growth rate can.
>
> Readit later via[Instapaper](https://us.list-manage.com/12_WdeaGkbW?e=86169b0ba2&c2id=4541cead0ded4f764194eec0f1f5b20f).
> Shareit via[Twitter](https://us.list-manage.com/YwvqtBwhStA?e=86169b0ba2&c2id=4541cead0ded4f764194eec0f1f5b20f)or[email](mailto:?subject=arr+doesn%27t+mean+what+it+used+to&body=Great post: https://signalsbysimon.substack.com/p/arr-doesnt-mean-what-it-used-to-15%0d%0a%0d%0a%0d%0aFound it via http://softwareleadweekly.com/).
>
> Peopleware
>
> ---------------------------------------------------------------
>
> [Escaping the LLM Coding Rat Race](https://us.list-manage.com/6RFJTBKATXA?e=86169b0ba2&c2id=4541cead0ded4f764194eec0f1f5b20f)
> 6 minutes read.
>
> Aaron Stannard checked whether the AI speed race is actually producing happier customers, and the numbers say no: "30% increase in total supply; 3% growth in utilization: this does not sound like customers clamoring for more LLM-authored applications." Is shipping speed really the bottleneck, or is it effective distribution (e.g., being paid for) and adoption?
>
> Readit later via[Instapaper](https://us.list-manage.com/BECnGgiqMnP?e=86169b0ba2&c2id=4541cead0ded4f764194eec0f1f5b20f).
> Shareit via[Twitter](https://us.list-manage.com/mTUTpFqDzIx?e=86169b0ba2&c2id=4541cead0ded4f764194eec0f1f5b20f)or[email](mailto:?subject=escaping+the+llm+coding+rat+race&body=Great post: https://aaronstannard.com/escaping-ai-coding-ratrace/%0d%0a%0d%0a%0d%0aFound it via http://softwareleadweekly.com/).
>
> [Data Lessons From Inside Meta](https://us.list-manage.com/R3AZ2t5tvb_?e=86169b0ba2&c2id=4541cead0ded4f764194eec0f1f5b20f)
> 13 minutes read.
>
> Great interview with Shridhar Iyer, sharing many observations and insights about working at a big company like Meta. One takeaway for me is around data quality: "But I would point to a few things Meta did earlier than others, and at scale. One was to schematize, both online and offline, strongly typing schemas and enforcing them all the way upstream to the log statement. That was a big undertaking, and it is what made column-level lineage possible, which was fundamental to the privacy work." Another takeaway is how much the entire industry is still in the discovery phase when it comes to AI: "Teams are becoming larger because the middle layer is being removed, and a lot of managers have been asked to become individual contributors." -- Can you actually lead a team that size (20-30 ICs) without weekly 1:1s?
>
> Readit later via[Instapaper](https://us.list-manage.com/YKb788haxa1?e=86169b0ba2&c2id=4541cead0ded4f764194eec0f1f5b20f).
> Shareit via[Twitter](https://us.list-manage.com/19hlhN9eu0I?e=86169b0ba2&c2id=4541cead0ded4f764194eec0f1f5b20f)or[email](mailto:?subject=data+lessons+from+inside+meta&body=Great post: https://roundup.getdbt.com/p/data-lessons-from-inside-meta-shridhar%0d%0a%0d%0a%0d%0aFound it via http://softwareleadweekly.com/).
>
> [Why Do I Write?](https://us.list-manage.com/UYEWe1Q4fxZ?e=86169b0ba2&c2id=4541cead0ded4f764194eec0f1f5b20f)
> 3 minutes read.
>
> Michael Lopp's real point: writing isn't just how he communicates, it's how he builds, and what breaks people is failing to build in some form, not failing to write. We all have our own version of that outlet, whether it's code, docs, or a Friday newsletter, and the ones who lose motivation are usually the ones who stopped treating it as building anything at all.
>
> Readit later via[Instapaper](https://us.list-manage.com/kIlRLnlQQgd?e=86169b0ba2&c2id=4541cead0ded4f764194eec0f1f5b20f).
> Shareit via[Twitter](https://us.list-manage.com/oKDL8BNqVOA?e=86169b0ba2&c2id=4541cead0ded4f764194eec0f1f5b20f)or[email](mailto:?subject=why+do+i+write%3f&body=Great post: https://randsinrepose.com/archives/the-motivation/%0d%0a%0d%0a%0d%0aFound it via http://softwareleadweekly.com/).
>
> And finally, inspiring tweets...
>
> ---------------------------------------------------------------
>
> [@paulg](https://us.list-manage.com/10qelZw7ER0?e=86169b0ba2&c2id=4541cead0ded4f764194eec0f1f5b20f): When you go talk to users, don't (just) ask them what features they want. Ask them what problems they have. In the best case this will lead you to think of features they'll love but would never have thought to ask for. And expect to go through multiple cycles of ask-and-build.
>
> [@readswithravi](https://us.list-manage.com/13_E538RPTK?e=86169b0ba2&c2id=4541cead0ded4f764194eec0f1f5b20f): James Clear: “You are not your grand plans. You are your daily patterns.”
>

July 16, 2026 | Read Online
<https://leadershipintech.com/newsletters/2322?sid=027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4>

How successful companies go blind
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22748/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*4 minutes* by Ian Reppel

Successful companies can lose the ability to recognize good engineering the
same way cave fish lost their sight: the environment stops rewarding the
trait, so it quietly disappears. New hires learn broken systems, then
replicate them through hiring panels, and careful engineers who push back
get overruled until they leave or adapt.

The AI Testing Gap Between Demo and Production
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22749/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*sponsored by BrowserStack*

A model that works in a demo and one your team trusts on release day are
two different things. At the QA Leadership Summit, leaders from companies
like Google Cloud and Salesforce walkthrough how they're putting AI into
testing, measuring its impact, and building reliable systems that scale.
Free virtual event, July 22.

Real-time feedback in every interview
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22750/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*10 minutes* by Mark Grebler

Ending interviews with direct, honest feedback turns a one-way process into
a real conversation. Candidates rarely get it, so they almost always
appreciate it. Giving specific feedback on candidates' capabilities also
allows them to correct genuine misunderstandings, leading to better hiring
decisions. Mark suggests focusing on what someone said or did, not their
personality traits.

How much should a manager code?
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22751/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*6 minutes* by Roman Nikolaev

Coding as a manager isn't about hitting a time percentage. It's about
staying connected to how your systems and team actually work. The real risk
isn't coding too much or too little — it's coding to carry delivery
yourself instead of building a team that runs without you. A green
contribution graph isn't the goal. A team that barely needs you is.

The laws of this world (game theory)
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22752/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*15 minutes* by Incentivising

Hard work alone rarely leads to success because effort without the right
position, visibility, and problem selection produces little return. The
market only rewards outcomes, not hours spent. Most people follow an
outdated model that no longer reflects how success actually works. Fixing
your direction, making your competence visible, and solving problems the
market genuinely needs will matter far more than working harder.

How do I deal with my team member resisting a change?
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22753/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*10 minutes* by Andi Roberts

Overt resistance to change is often more useful than it looks. The person
objecting out loud is still engaged, and their pushback frequently signals
a flaw in the plan, an unacknowledged cost, or simply exhaustion from too
many changes at once. Research shows that perceived fairness, not
enthusiasm or persuasion, most reliably reduces resistance.

July 26, 2026 | Read Online
<https://leadershipintech.com/newsletters/2331?sid=027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4>

The 37% rule
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22819/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*2 minutes* by Michał Poczwardowski

There is a mathematical strategy to help make better decisions when
choosing from a sequence of options you cannot revisit. It works by
spending the first 37% of options on observation only, then picking the
next option that beats everything seen so far. This gives you roughly a 1
in 3 chance of finding the best option. The key lesson is simple: calibrate
long enough to set a benchmark, but not so long that you miss your best
choice.

[Webinar] Can you prove AI is working?
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22820/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*sponsored by Unblocked*

AI is in your engineering workflow. While the token spend shows it, the
throughput doesn't. The human is very much still in the loop, and that's a
context problem. Join live on Aug 19 (FREE)
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22821/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
to learn how to track AI productivity, how context maturity affects where
teams stall, and what it takes to get real value from your agents.

Perfection is not over-engineering
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22822/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*4 minutes* by Var Zero

Over-engineering means solving the wrong problem, not caring too much about
quality. A perfect solution exists when requirements are clear enough that
only one answer fits. The real culprit behind bloated systems is poor
requirements gathering, not ambition. Get every constraint on the table and
the right solution becomes obvious.

Effectiveness comes from developing both leadership and management skills
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22823/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*6 minutes* by James Samuel

Leadership and management aren't opposites — they're both necessary.
Leadership creates direction and inspires movement, while management turns
that movement into real results. One without the other leads to either
vision without execution or busy work without purpose. The good news is
that you're already practicing both, since every time you influence others
or make smart use of limited resources, you're doing exactly that.

Make no assumptions
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22824/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*9 minutes* by Will Larson

Codebases often build up distinct layers over time as new developers add
code without fully understanding what came before. AI tools are making this
worse, and the same problem is now spreading to how teams reason and make
decisions. AI-generated analysis can look polished while being built on
flawed data, leading teams to solve problems that don't exist. The fix is
to inspect every layer of reasoning rather than accepting it at face value.

Avoid the trap of magical thinking
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22825/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*4 minutes* by Bjorn Roche

Good leadership isn't about inspiring speeches or chasing productivity
hacks. It's about understanding real business challenges, removing
bottlenecks, and focusing teams on what actually matters. Magical thinking
lets leaders avoid responsibility, so when things fail, they blame effort
rather than direction. Strong teams built on clear goals and good
communication outperform collections of star individuals.

August 06, 2026 | Read Online
<https://leadershipintech.com/newsletters/2337?sid=027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4>

Reflecting on my failure to build a billion-dollar company
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22903/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*16 minutes* by Sahil Lavingia

Gumroad started as a weekend project that quickly attracted millions in
venture funding, but growth stalled and led to laying off 75% of the team.
Rather than shut down, the founder chose to keep the business running for
the creators who depended on it. Over time, the company became lean and
profitable. Sahil now measures success by the value created for others, not
by chasing a billion-dollar valuation.

Learn how leading teams are building and scaling AI agents in production
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22904/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*sponsored by LangChain*

Agents can write and run code seconds before execution, often without human
review.  Local environments and standard containers weren’t designed for
that level of autonomy. LangChain’s latest guide explains what safe agent
execution requires, including isolation, permissions, observability, fast
provisioning, and persistent state. Read the guide to learn when a managed
sandbox makes sense, what capabilities to look for, and how to give agents
the tools they need without handing them your infrastructure.

Two-way doors sometimes lock behind you
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22905/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*5 minutes* by Jeff

Technical decisions that seem reversible can become difficult to undo over
time. As teams build more systems, processes, and dependencies around a
technology, the cost of replacing it increases. Jeff explains that
optionality must be actively maintained, not simply assumed. He suggests
documenting assumptions, estimating migration costs, testing exit plans,
and accepting when some decisions are worth making permanent.

Finding calm in life and work
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22906/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*8 minutes* by Yew Jin Lim

Anxiety measurably hurts performance, dropping the average anxious person
to the bottom third of their peers. Modern work culture makes this worse by
rewarding busyness over real output, pushing people toward low value
activity that triggers constant dopamine seeking and low grade stress. Yew
suggests simple habits that help: set clear work boundaries, move your
body, spend time with people, and focus on work that actually matters to
you. Calm is not a reward for finishing. It is available now.

A practical guide to fast onboarding
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22907/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*15 minutes* by Steve Huynh

Getting up to speed in a new role feels overwhelming because people treat
all information the same. A better approach is to sort incoming knowledge
into three buckets: facts, processes, and concepts. Facts you either
memorize or know where to find. Processes are best learned by doing.
Concepts, the ideas behind how things connect, are the most powerful
because they transfer across roles and domains, letting you contribute
faster wherever you go.

How to measure engineering
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22908/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*16 minutes* by Jade Rubick

ValueSum is a metric for engineering teams that tracks value delivered over
time. You list everything shipped, score each item by business value, and
sum it up. Higher scores mean faster delivery of more valuable work. It
works across product, engineering, and design, can be applied
retroactively, and helps teams learn by rescoring features months later to
see how predictions compared to reality.

August 09, 2026 | Read Online
<https://leadershipintech.com/newsletters/2341?sid=027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4>

Before you delegate
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22930/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*7 minutes* by Wes Kao

Delegating is hard when explaining a task feels slower than just doing it
yourself. Wes shares a simple checklist of six questions that makes it
easier: understand what the person already knows, share the why, gather
needed resources, show what good looks like, set a clear timeline, and flag
likely risks. These questions help build shared context fast and improve
the quality of work handed off.

[Webinar] Measuring AI developer productivity: Can you prove AI is working?
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22931/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*sponsored by Unblocked*

AI is in your engineering workflow. While the token spend shows it, the
throughput doesn't. The human is very much still in the loop, and that's a
context problem. Join live on Aug 19 (FREE)
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22932/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
to learn how to track AI productivity, how context maturity affects where
teams stall, and what it takes to get real value from your agents.

Overpromising
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22933/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*6 minutes* by Stay SaaSy

Promising career rewards to keep team members happy often backfires badly.
The initial boost fades fast, and if the promise falls through, trust is
shattered and can take years to rebuild. Managers should only make promises
they can fully guarantee, and caveat anything uncertain with honest
disclaimers. Focus on what you can control, not what you hope will happen.

Engineering management after the cost of code collapsed
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22934/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*13 minutes* by Karim Jedda

Engineering management rules aren't uniformly obsolete or valid in the AI
era. The key is checking what assumption each practice rests on. If a rule
depends on the cost of writing code, revisit it. If it depends on how
humans coordinate, verify, or accept risk, it still holds. Code generation
got cheap, but specification, judgment, and ownership did not. Whoever has
to sign a decision still sets the pace.

When nothing happens
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22935/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*12 minutes* by Mike Fisher

Organizations don't punish bad behavior — they punish bad behavior when
results turn. Research shows that whether a harsh leader is seen as
inspiring or abusive depends mostly on whether the team is winning. Cases
like Steve Jobs, Linda Wachner, and Bobby Knight all follow the same
pattern: the behavior stays constant, but the moment results drop,
tolerance evaporates. The real question for any leader is how much cover
strong performance is currently buying for someone on their team.

The lights were never off
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22936/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*14 minutes* by Ilko Kacharov

The idea of the dark factory promises software development with little or
no human involvement. But the teams closest to this work tell a more
nuanced story. Humans still review, verify, and guide AI-generated code.
The real challenge is not removing people, but replacing the work reviewers
do with strong tests, simulations, automation, and safeguards.

Laws of project management
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22733/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*11 minutes* by Lucas F. Costa

Lucas presents ten practical laws for managing projects successfully. He
explains that projects succeed by solving real problems, adapting to new
information, and delivering working solutions early. The laws emphasize
flexible planning, clear priorities, teamwork, operational readiness, and
testing important assumptions first. Together, they provide simple guidance
for building products that meet user needs while balancing business goals,
time, scope, and quality.

[Webinar] 8 levels of context maturity in AI-native development
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22734/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*sponsored by Unblocked*

AI is in your engineering workflow. While the token spend shows it, the
throughput doesn't. The human is very much still in the loop, and that's a
context problem. Join live (FREE) on Jul 23
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22735/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>to
see the 8 levels of context maturity: where most teams are stuck, what the
ceiling looks like at each stage, and what it actually takes to make the
most out of your agents.

Notes on the software factory
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22736/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*6 minutes* by Benedict Brady

AI coding agents work best when given real tools like browser access,
deployment pipelines, and test runners. Adding a critic agent to review
work improves quality, and pairing that with more thinking time on hard
problems helps further. Memory and long-running workflows are still weak
points. The whole stack, from cloud infrastructure to IDEs, needs to be
rebuilt around agents rather than adapted from human-centered tools.

How do I create accountability without authority?
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22737/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*22 minutes* by Andi Roberts

Cross-functional leaders often assume that once a commitment is made in a
meeting, it will hold. It rarely does without deliberate structure to
support it. Accountability in a matrix organisation must be built through
precise agreements, visible records, and early conversations about
competing priorities. The goal is habits the whole group owns, not just the
leader, so accountability survives long after any single person moves on.

The Mario meeting
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22738/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*5 minutes* by Michael Lopp

Companies plan compensation budgets more than a year in advance, long
before anyone sees their review. At any given time, two budgets run at
once: one being spent now, one being built for next year. Senior leaders
must understand this full process, not just the end result. Most employees
only see the final number, but the real decisions happen much earlier, in
rooms most people never enter.

Raise the ambition threshold
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22739/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*4 minutes* by Jack Vanlightly

Building software faster with AI doesn't mean building more software. Every
new system adds ongoing costs to operate, secure, and maintain, and too
many obligations can crowd out the capacity to build what truly matters. AI
should raise the bar for what teams attempt, not lower the bar for what
gets built. Companies that use AI only to clear backlogs risk being
overtaken by bolder competitors tackling harder, more valuable problems.

How to write an effective software design document
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22690/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*19 minutes* by Michael Lynch

Writing a design doc before coding saves time by forcing clear thinking and
helping teams coordinate. It should focus on high-stakes decisions where
getting things wrong is costly, not minor choices that are easy to fix
later. Key sections include goals, background, diagrams, security, and open
issues. The right length depends on project complexity, team size, and risk.

Understand cost, adoption, and productivity of your org's AI coding agents
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22691/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*sponsored by Dash0*

Bills are skyrocketing but engineering leaders are flying blind. AI Coding
Insights from Dash0 connects token spend to what is actually produced. Cost
per merged PR, adoption across teams, and cycle time per model, with a
drill-down from any number to the exact agent session behind it. Works with
Claude Code and Cursor. Free until August.

Still writing tests manually? Meticulous AI is here.
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22692/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*sponsored by Meticulous*

Most teams are forced to make the tradeoff between better coverage and more
maintenance effort. But top engineering teams like Notion, Dropbox,
Wealthsimple and LaunchDarkly have discovered a new testing paradigm. Built
by ex-Palantir engineers, Meticulous creates and maintains E2E UI tests
that covers every edge case of your web app without any developer effort -
making it the only tool to improve both product quality and dev velocity.

The social physics of conversation
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22693/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*10 minutes* by Andi Roberts

Research from MIT found that how groups communicate matters more than what
they say. Tracking tone, turn-taking, and who speaks to whom predicted team
performance better than intelligence or talent combined. Groups where
conversation flows freely between all members consistently outperform those
where exchanges funnel through one central person.

How to ask for help from people who don't know you
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22694/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*5 minutes* by Pradyu Prasad

Asking for help is a skill anyone can learn. The key is to think from the
other person's perspective. Make yourself worth helping by showing real
proof of your work, keep your context brief, and make your request small
and specific. Also make it easy for them to say no, since willing help
builds lasting relationships while forced help destroys them.

The four pillars of engineering management
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22695/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*9 minutes* by James Samuel

Engineering managers exist because growing organizations need coordination,
alignment, and direction that flat structures cannot sustain. The role
varies by company and context, but the core job stays the same: make the
team effective. This breaks down into four areas — leading people, guiding
technical decisions, shaping product thinking, and driving delivery.
Success means understanding what blocks the team and removing those
obstacles.

How Meta sets up super IC teams
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22696/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*5 minutes* by Yue Zhao

Small, high-powered teams of experienced individual contributors are
gaining popularity at companies trying to launch new products or transform
operations. These "super IC teams" work best with four things in place: an
executive sponsor with broad influence, full team dedication with no split
responsibilities, a sharp focus on a real customer problem, and a handoff
plan before scaling becomes urgent. Without these conditions, teams waste
time on politics and lose momentum.

August 02, 2026 | Read Online
<https://leadershipintech.com/newsletters/2336?sid=027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4>

Framework for managers starting a new role
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22882/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*10 minutes* by James Samuel

Starting strong in a new management role is less about moving fast and more
about building the right foundation. Focus on absorbing company culture,
building relationships early, and aligning with your manager on clear
expectations. Resist the urge to prove yourself immediately. Instead,
listen to your team, spot patterns, and let your first wins come from
solving problems that actually matter.

A simple view on progressive software architecture
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22883/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*sponsored by IcePanel*

The way software is built is changing. Yet, architecture thinking is more
important than ever. Learn about key principles and frameworks we believe
are critical to thriving in this new, chaotic world.

The AI productivity gap
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22884/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*3 minutes* by Bjorn Roche

AI speeds up coding, but coding is only a small part of most developers'
days. Senior engineers spend most of their time in meetings, reviews, and
system design, areas where AI helps little. This means AI saves seniors
around 15% of their time, while juniors, who code more, gain closer to 25%.
Expect steady improvements, but not dramatic productivity leaps, especially
from your most experienced staff.

How to think about span of control
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22885/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*18 minutes* by Jade Rubick

Span of control and team size involve real tradeoffs. Fewer management
layers reduce complexity and improve clarity, but overloaded managers
neglect their teams and slow everything down. Small teams of around eight
people tend to move faster and work better together, while larger teams
offer more resilience. As companies grow and processes mature, spans of
control can increase, but mandates pushing managers to oversee 20 or more
people rarely work well in practice.

No wind is a good wind
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22886/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*5 minutes* by Aviv Ben-Yosef

Teams move fast but build the wrong things when leadership lacks a clear
vision. There's a simple check: ask yourself what the vision is, then ask a
few engineers the same question and see if the answers match. If the vision
exists but you're not part of those conversations, push your way into the
right rooms. If it doesn't exist at all, that's a problem worth raising
loudly.

The economic benefit of refactoring
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22887/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*16 minutes* by Giles Edwards-Alexander

An AI agent built a 150,000-line application, but its data access layer
ballooned into a single 17,000-line file with massive repetition. To test
whether refactoring helps AI agents work more efficiently, the same code
change was measured before and after each refactoring step. After 15 steps,
the tokens needed to make that change dropped by 83%, because the agent
could read fewer, better-organized files instead of scanning one giant one.

Online Wednesday, July 22 at 5CT

Aaron Gallant breaks down MCP Factory, a repository that lets you spin up
any MCP server
<https://link.mail.beehiiv.com/ss/c/u001.3a5P_SwQzY5x8USD2q4p0vHb8GdcjonmgvPcPB9XhFMDdVI1o_GtDhuc7TimzwJdcjZzJeWmzhM46tdeEQcNNX-Lv_WZERf8JkcpW8TV7TX7sqS1Bb998JFhNuMrFHzAAKDCIAaqi1A8kaRVq5cO1eyvzfN75qTQfKTytp4jSjYd5C2MSQ9al_0Y9XckGOefa46Nu92zN1w18aUCucruOqcg-WhZvH9u3G-dE4H31nqgILvs2s_Msm_zrWPAy_DuDs9IJNJGoI1ch-b91Yyucg/4se/fAcrFrAxSxK8wmcqipcb6Q/h4/h001.LGWAh1CFLcOFNmE4KLsKmq5DTSIs7e9cqIOMBBtcCjo>
you want instead of hand-building each integration from scratch. MCP
servers are how AI agents plug into real tools and data, and Aaron walks
through how the repo is structured, how to go from an idea to a working MCP
fast, and how generating them changes what you can wire your agents into.
With the next cohort approaching, it's also a clear look at the kind of
work Gauntlet
<https://link.mail.beehiiv.com/ss/c/u001.3a5P_SwQzY5x8USD2q4p0vHb8GdcjonmgvPcPB9XhFMDdVI1o_GtDhuc7TimzwJdcjZzJeWmzhM46tdeEQcNNX-Lv_WZERf8JkcpW8TV7TX7sqS1Bb998JFhNuMrFHzAAKDCIAaqi1A8kaRVq5cO1eyvzfN75qTQfKTytp4jSjbGGt1uEdjDJ0ma16lCGvrIW8BMMKSMvPJUwotvTCFYdVnMtsppQ6iiIVjpaGhnVxJsf7uPzYQkVK5xLqihhPyhCUJu9P94VWJfh1Lk-yw8OQ/4se/fAcrFrAxSxK8wmcqipcb6Q/h5/h001.FRFk6CYQcLpcJzqgdbdX2KHOfD470dydkjNtnFPTQuI>
engineers do every day.

You'll learn:

   -

   Why MCP servers matter for connecting agents to real tools and data
   -

   How MCP Factory is structured as a reusable repository
   -

   How to go from an idea to a working MCP quickly

How Do I Deal With My Team Members Who Are Resisting Change?
<https://link.mail.beehiiv.com/ss/c/u001.K3FzaWG5vnes9asChrWWUKbMMnqUeYWqSu5-9c8LbTeZXJRI2O5thYj0NhJlLLBKey8J1_Zbwz5kQTOWdhnV8dMeYHbSj33VvB3Ya5MQWS7lqDOQnmAqjvy5zu_ZvDzkUR6gf7JfsMP2bjQcwzXv84CRPhp6rC-biAibUTXOZ-6ZqHTEVh_HyE4aTSDNPQpRWUCOyBy1vbwAT6Vd_1aSZQ/4se/fAcrFrAxSxK8wmcqipcb6Q/h7/h001.F6SN0AHd5XXEo54B4q3K-FeIOHUbQk6lwc1pih1Rau0>

— Andi Roberts

*tl;dr*: “Every leader eventually meets the same person. Arms folded in the
meeting. The one who says “we tried this before” before you have finished
the sentence. The one whose email replies get a little shorter each time
the project is mentioned. It is tempting to label this person difficult,
negative, or stuck. It is also, usually, the wrong diagnosis.”
* Management ConflictResolution*

Code Yellow, Code Red
<https://link.mail.beehiiv.com/ss/c/u001.NxDti-hLnxnUue0yjhAbRz14sG_Qqg3mQcec1wBSAw7E2jnDzSLarfCKrDYcJ5u9OXqTAF3o1eTJqchSshP93DIhRsTMwUfWCxy8bABwKPxsvvD7dKO9IkN7aEzGxiMD9O152OJPQYBjLr-gzecFUTaD6OpLpMQa6IfNm2lmkG9a35unQL6IV_FsuroeyXfV/4se/fAcrFrAxSxK8wmcqipcb6Q/h8/h001.M2MCCsXORp27WKiv-6cAtus_vEsSF9qty-MccpCzKvw>

— James Stanier

*tl;dr*: Here’s what we’re going to cover: (1) What Code Yellow and Code
Red actually mean, and where the terms come from. (2) How we ran ours at
Provet: the structure, the work, and what we learned. (3) A generalised
template you can adapt for your own organisation. (4) When to call one,
when to escalate, and the failure modes to watch for.
* Management Incidents CaseStudy*

*Events AI Agents *

What Does “Playing Politics" Mean For Software Engineers?
<https://link.mail.beehiiv.com/ss/c/u001.3a5P_SwQzY5x8USD2q4p0nlI2N4DuGMKadU0rdUBb5FSYaFZtVH7eOSp33MhyjEGBY-ac97JDWZRM7Q7gIeF3LxPr3bTmrjctMexNDObgO-6iufMd56RI8c76AQURu3jpRQls6kUooLN-fH5yjDn-9FZwBjs_MedtMrV4GZ5Z74/4se/fAcrFrAxSxK8wmcqipcb6Q/h10/h001.F1PxhLFqB-WuX4HRhcQwmlxHhEtKHrww18Wdaw3DXzA>

— Sean Goedecke

*tl;dr*: “Playing politics isn’t about plotting and scheming, and it isn’t
just about being a friendly, likeable person (although that helps). It’s
about figuring out how your company actually operates: who makes the
decisions, who gets consulted, what behavior gets rewarded, and so on. The
most basic way to do that is to figure out who is powerful, get out of
their way, and (if you can) help them get what they want.”
*CareerGrowth Influence *

"The successful warrior is the average man, with laser-like focus.”

– Bruce Lee
Reverse Engineering ChatGPT Web: How OpenAI Built for a Billion Users
<https://link.mail.beehiiv.com/ss/c/u001.PFjHD-ii0vvUj1FcNnOS5rTDEkKrv2aS0v9dB9vLzyKQDC19NcVH8ZjP7D_1idlsN0_AMVlFe15IyskJmaNyJKAsMTClnWrp73OAVrzGQj3bIbtFBrRJ8HeveBuJFdpr40ReNZv76zW-I2VxC1Atwg/4se/fAcrFrAxSxK8wmcqipcb6Q/h11/h001.7VQNqL5uA_5Qb4Eajs_okIW2SMY43E6M_AHbUnk72TU>

— Dennis Brotzky

*tl;dr*: “On the surface it seems like a simple interface, but after
digging in it's anything but. I spent days reverse engineering their web
app by digging through the page source, bundled code, and network requests
to understand how it was built.”
*DeepDive AI Architecture*

Why Stitched-Together AI Architectures Backfire
<https://link.mail.beehiiv.com/ss/c/u001.TDQMwwTUsDB62J0OSnrlnNOWXz98F1nhh8ThNWDeGkcmPW71OQnnI82SUoHPvMiK36H8lGk96bdQqcyBQTdGVo507pC2jW9DM1JLStbRPMlUm-SP5zKhdafTUUYmivOxNTOWnRal1_yHs3cJBSeyyw/4se/fAcrFrAxSxK8wmcqipcb6Q/h12/h001.B4bAok9iDa77WFfpvA__pbvpW-axVitcUh9pzfsgmpI>

— Chanh Tran

*tl;dr*: Building context and memory for production AI agents usually means
stitching together a fragile tool zoo of vector dbs, sync pipelines, and
external session caches. This fragmented architecture breeds massive
engineering overhead. Redis Iris fixes this by unifying context
orchestration, persistent agent memory, and semantic caching into a single,
low-latency platform. Try the hands-on tutorial.

*Promoted by Redis*
*DeepDive AI Architecture*

Benford's Law: The Strange Law That Catches Financial Fraudsters
<https://link.mail.beehiiv.com/ss/c/u001.sGoRjeV3QKXxxm_cll-jKWgG6zJaXsilHOCwniCaXc9-7_tm69yjpapkhxybhGnQjDmNwbpp6MYgQ_F3eMmiFM40m65yEcgXJCwlognJnv7R1YoSS6JEUF6LPHmBaE3hEPLOBibN1vOsWVkNY2xi9XpWlbhdJxHuxVJDfd2bCjA/4se/fAcrFrAxSxK8wmcqipcb6Q/h13/h001.CZbSJ4T1yrQXm60rEbQXjMpBNYOsNUsPPbjaNvO5Em8>

— Vatsal Bakshi

*tl;dr*: In almost any large dataset drawn from the real world —
populations of cities, lengths of rivers, prices, earthquake depths,
company revenues — the leading digit is not uniformly distributed. The
digit 1 appears as the first digit about 30% of the time. Not 11%. Nearly
three times the naive expectation. The digit 9 appears less than 5% of the
time.
*Security Algo*

Quadrupling Code Performance With A “Useless" If
<https://link.mail.beehiiv.com/ss/c/u001.PFjHD-ii0vvUj1FcNnOS5plHTHQS1vuOeV0uMxsJptIchyTUl-NzY4rj-jsOHEOam2aePCfgdJWdmcmTkAyYrEMvo5ynsOBWMIZFLc92N1QzC0UhoF7kkseCPhtj3yYrjUbnO7JdL0SAY0BvNXj3RfQ8nTeD4dHXLLJ7VkhOClw0U3jCtlQ4WQIgyKmAb_ISuANKhhIrNV2eKK3xaCwquQ/4se/fAcrFrAxSxK8wmcqipcb6Q/h15/h001.a8mR46j72SwtBYoVXcaoyHNy36ziaKMa56srl7-WxZY>

— Alisa Sireneva

*tl;dr*: “We don’t expect too many chunks, so next_j[i][j] is quite likely
to just be equal to j. If we could tell the CPU to predict that j stays
intact, the loop would become throughput-bound rather than latency-bound.”

### Fwd: Issue #735
_aiste.ulozaite@gmail.com_

- Newsletter roundup covers leadership communication (tailoring messages to execs, staff, and customers), project management laws, and 'competence blindness' in successful companies
- Featured technical pieces include defending partial codebase understanding, DFS pattern for trees/graphs, and a resilient-systems series on handling time
- Case study: Bitso cut change failure rate 83% and boosted deploy frequency 2.1x using Signadot sandboxes for 250+ engineers and coding agents across 200+ microservices
- Promoted content includes a free webinar on '8 Levels of Context Maturity in AI-Native Engineering' (July 23) and Unblocked's context engine for engineering teams

A Small Problem
<https://link.mail.beehiiv.com/ss/c/u001.u7AEjFG7xD7PeXgObD5uI1o6BtYZXOmqEviUlhsqJwh5AVQIa6Tht9vCY02wzMBPG2OWhpuSeG1pH6gI70LJkOgW3ReFcJi_KtU0UzohCzbpU9MCaTidx0qcYDBL23iT2-mX0QAOUlSsNJVFFgDqrqMVoNt1t0FcaBv_zTMTJp4/4si/c3Fv450_TjO6jsHLxm7gVg/h7/h001.ZRD_nVevPDKIz5eLkppH4jT9-orWBqhsFNI-VAwFXsQ>

— Mike Fisher

*tl;dr*: “In an organization you are always broadcasting on more than one
channel at once, whether you intend to or not. Your exec team needs the
unvarnished situation. The wider company needs enough to stay oriented
without spinning out. Customers need a different message again. The failure
mode is not the absence of a calm voice. It is using one voice for
everyone: either feeding the cockpit a soothing story or dumping the
cockpit’s panic straight into the cabin. Calm is contagious, and so is
fear. Whichever one you transmit is the one that spreads.”
* Leadership Management Communication*

Lucas' Laws of Project Management
<https://link.mail.beehiiv.com/ss/c/u001.3a5P_SwQzY5x8USD2q4p0iXWukwtzo0n-HZhmbKC-6IH6S2lm2PJQxO77ai2ImAicfw-LCr2fACBiKDFcS8OblcuB8yl3X1OVPDFHHPEtY_1_aPQ8iU1C3RcPilkDRBssVNKZZbn3Ef9pqi5Dh36fMH1NDCCVzLwfxuxch-CG15nC3_dGG1Z1BRaaW8gbtbR_APbnegeozOOnvwAoJdKIQ/4si/c3Fv450_TjO6jsHLxm7gVg/h8/h001.dRSylJ-XQ9y2GzkxMIKndEzAnq2GP1L6m2_Zhma9SWg>

— Lucas Costa

*tl;dr*: (1) A project succeeds when it solves the problem. Compliance with
the original specification is merely one possible way to get there. (2)
Scope cannot be known before the work begins. It can only become less wrong
as the work proceeds. (3) A fixed date requires variable scope. A fixed
scope requires a variable date. Pretending both variables are fixed does
not make it so. And more.
* Management Tips*

[Webinar] 8 Levels Of Context Maturity In AI-Native Engineering
<https://link.mail.beehiiv.com/ss/c/u001.3a5P_SwQzY5x8USD2q4p0ifl3M68qmkH56j-n_NuhhP4hNYBzzelAmkV9t-_1NZM3CnK9myqMAOuvxPOjuhbd4gIqzbRv-DDCvI8kMn2q1A6Pllob_vXO6GW5w2GKr5Xex_y9aDXHqj7yLqI2x7ArI_N4w8r3wLfbq5Gfz64-4KD7kLtm-7LYJTLfxWLXk5Xfcek1NB77VuKat_N70ixm8HZG0OLP677SyXp-0FA5NDWtqLU_mOisFh4q2nKEBe_/4si/c3Fv450_TjO6jsHLxm7gVg/h9/h001.JAQfbyWCCjKCbVs5LxpRV3OYN03lp-qL_l-Hs78-d4U>

*tl;dr*: AI is in your engineering workflow. While the token spend shows
it, the throughput doesn't. The human is very much still in the loop, and
that's a context problem. This free webinar maps the 8 levels of context
maturity: where most teams are stuck, what the ceiling looks like at each
stage, and what it actually takes to make the most out of your agents. Join
live July 23 (FREE).

*Promoted by Unblocked*
*Events AI Agents *

How Successful Companies Go Blind
<https://link.mail.beehiiv.com/ss/c/u001.GWllEGwX7fbrlXfChzlURqc0yGRH1oFUfM9uHWImYDHYbb17FpsTGoSInLkPtk4vmdjKuSeGSWI5xeOktqD5973jxQ2UY56WiMbkxaLw7L6x2Arh3-A90eUibI1NcuyLJgVjQYYMJUMQl1RpJPgNuU84Gvi7TFyZMxZKSqF-5-QPJaNMc0IePetStVOwA_iZ/4si/c3Fv450_TjO6jsHLxm7gVg/h10/h001.JDCwnizboDYrG7dYfMvyiNc182MDGGem39CYgFgStow>

— Ian Reppel

*tl;dr*: “Companies who have forgotten what it took to become successful
are similar: they stop recognizing competence, because the environment has
stopped expressing the trait in anyone the company hires. Call it
competence blindness, which is different from incumbents who fail because
they cling to the customers and margins of yesterday’s market. Firms with
competence blindness do not disappear. In fact, they can survive for
decades.”
*Leadership Culture Strategy *

"The most important thing in communication is to hear what isn't being
said."

– Peter Drucker
In Defense Of Not Understanding Your Codebase
<https://link.mail.beehiiv.com/ss/c/u001.3a5P_SwQzY5x8USD2q4p0nlI2N4DuGMKadU0rdUBb5Gg-4QYrBe0vpRmoDuzhbTl29js0WeSQ4O78KmfMLO6ldor--IJsAiO8Ad0luv5ura6-KPxStICGdL0X0QD_5HQhUrKKLN966sJd10MiDwm8GvBE99dbME0TUkc1249Kn8XNMJn23EPnMusYZyT4bmfVwfRc_SYD4RjLUOU0vnneA/4si/c3Fv450_TjO6jsHLxm7gVg/h11/h001.mpt7embXwNUZGcfDTtXIDXcTZMZtKt7QWh6Ec58X-II>

— Sean Goedeckey

*tl;dr*: “These are two largely different ways of programming with
different methods, practices and cultures. However, the first group is
over-represented in online discussion about software engineering. I want to
defend the second group against the first. In many software engineering
environments, there’s nothing wrong with being in a state of partial
understanding. In fact, in large systems a partial understanding is the
best you can do.”
*CareerGrowth CodeQuality*

How Bitso Cut Change Failure Rate 83% While Scaling Delivery With Coding
Agents
<https://link.mail.beehiiv.com/ss/c/u001.3a5P_SwQzY5x8USD2q4p0m895QuVtqvkW11dSkgIg8I7yY6GUJWSXHFbDSHBQ6GNQrjjAo0cEFcRihee9TwhCcKFwXp9kWqArYw-1vKhLC5-SsA8V6KgMm9AiCiXv5o_kXXftqId81GejAQnlxNvwOcWG_rx-MjxBAo7PE2xxKO_ezUQY_eGiIH32EH0LmCK8EP3dG_u-u42b4Cav2j51nnfyb7SgZRujmMPhAdlwfc/4si/c3Fv450_TjO6jsHLxm7gVg/h12/h001.kYlinAwQZCOKQjf6mAXxz0oGvVx8vs3vLmTjRzumNeA>

*tl;dr*: Bitso's 250+ engineers and their coding agents push changes across
200+ microservices, and shared staging was always broken. They replaced
full-stack preview environments with Signadot sandboxes that fork only the
changed services per PR. The result: 83% lower change failure rate, 2.1x
deploy frequency, and open-to-deploy down from 12.1 days to 4.6 days.

*Promoted by Signadot*
*CaseStudy Agents*

Don't Ask What You Want. Ask Who You Want To Be
<https://link.mail.beehiiv.com/ss/c/u001.T-cngy-HiLjOztVyxmCjHAiI2yPEX7vUHq-Jru9d4NuIS4jc7RuS31-h8F94faCB2_oeRiAcYGb4BMA6zXeoaJXh-dB_tBvS7iOLIX6f8X-QcmSdxnT_zem_2GNpPAcIoX0UkMGJwZiI_6WkfWlkbQXpjV5ny3vRD4ODUEonbuP8Gm3yc8szRjk61IdDXW8i/4si/c3Fv450_TjO6jsHLxm7gVg/h13/h001.4N6_q6E2okjtcWXQMfQFUnAUx8KvZtUzVLynIS1WVkQ>

— Doug Turnbull

*tl;dr*: In almost any large dataset drawn from the real world —
populations of cities, lengths of rivers, prices, earthquake depths,
company revenues — the leading digit is not uniformly distributed. The
digit 1 appears as the first digit about 30% of the time. Not 11%. Nearly
three times the naive expectation. The digit 9 appears less than 5% of the
time.
*CareerGrowth Wellbeing*

*tl;dr*: “This series is about making your programs more resilient. Each
post looks at one part of the system we take for granted: networks, time,
databases, storage, users, dependencies, deployments, queues, and
concurrency. The question is always the same: what assumptions are we
making here, and what should we do when they stop being true?”

handful of folks as the new team leads struggle, it’s easy to end up with a
complex code horizon very quickly.”

### Fwd: Issue #736
_aiste.ulozaite@gmail.com_

- Newsletter roundup for engineering leaders covering AI code review, developer experience, and leadership topics
- Featured tool: CodeRabbit Review restructures PR reviews into layer-by-layer walkthroughs with cohorts, semantic diff, and inline diagrams instead of flat file lists
- Articles explore AI's impact on productivity vs. developer experience, handling ambiguity in leadership, prefactoring code before new features, and why agent-driven coding needs better isolated environments (e.g. Signadot)
- Technical deep dives included on SIMD fundamentals and how Git commit sizes are calculated

*tl;dr*: AI writes more code than ever. Reviewing it shouldn’t mean
scrolling forty files in alphabetical order. CodeRabbit Review reorganizes
any pull request from a flat file list into a structured, layer-by-layer
walkthrough - the logical reading order of the change, not the order your
platform happens to sort it. Every range gets its own plain-language
summary, with sequence diagrams, state machines, and ERDs generated inline
wherever a visual earns its place. (1) Cohorts group related files and
chunks so you review one idea at a time. (2) Layers order them so
foundational changes - data shapes, contracts - come before the code that
depends on them. (3) Code Peek lets you click any variable, function, class
or type to see its definition and usages without leaving the tab. (4)
Semantic Diff cuts past formatting noise to show what actually changed.

tools is the goal of a toolmaker. One habit I see a lot, and have to push

game” which is “fun” to solve. I don’t want my tools to be “fun”. I want my
tools to be invisible.”

<https://link.mail.beehiiv.com/ss/c/u001.NxDti-hLnxnUue0yjhAbR9ihDugbmr0sv--A_jFIRE3EayKXEu62IIdTGAjdYDuNoONyUgwxz5LlNC1hhF1KaH1TGAVJUk8lfCpAXJOkHqpPzye6KAGMdLe_UVrfzve8dfhhOsJhxnjs1x3sSbnqOXrCyEL0S9ETG8IW-fxNYxuN9srHRbQlBxrjoTRASu14OZ8qaYvw6s0joCSwXqCwxQ/4sl/h7_ivzkxSm-YefoUD5CPxQ/h14/h001.J56ZXSgwo2a2fBW64fh5ppm0jBSqO8vCJYtnFsbhNYs>

— Rahul Singal

*tl;dr*: “Prefactoring (short for "preparatory refactoring") is the
practice of reworking existing code to make it more suitable for an
upcoming change before you actually implement the new functionality.
Instead of cleaning up code as an afterthought or trying to force a new
feature into an incompatible structure, you restructure the codebase
first.”
*Testing Guide*

- CodeRabbit Review (sponsor) restructures PR reviews into layer-by-layer walkthroughs with cohorts, code peek, and semantic diff instead of flat file lists.
- Featured essays cover prioritization pitfalls, staff engineer problem-finding, refactoring economics for AI agents, and scaling knowledge bases past 150 people.
- Deep dives include how Anthropic's software development is evolving with AI-driven code review, plus technical breakdowns of Linear's performance and the data tools landscape.

AI writes more code than ever. Reviewing it shouldn’t mean scrolling forty
files in alphabetical order.

CodeRabbit Review
<https://link.mail.beehiiv.com/ss/c/u001.Ira8ofUIq_P_lCLURPEgQ8Dok6h2-kZwEtf4UdHH35iVYNpxwNloseAvm_a3Lm94OCsq4ZNX6uwUrpuf71FQMuISU4n9nwwvCA-obagsOHwfZ6sNxRoeVD5wxFW819CEPujMztJVYpYBGlDZBqNPOA/4ss/FlauVPTqTeGKDEKpox9WIg/h4/h001.sDSjGcr8pcGqggPEnh192avzMf_5BnwEiwOaAcE8K3o>
reorganizes any pull request from a flat file list into a structured,
layer-by-layer walkthrough
<https://link.mail.beehiiv.com/ss/c/u001.Ira8ofUIq_P_lCLURPEgQ8Dok6h2-kZwEtf4UdHH35juzrTM5nS8s4eqXU-L9hRRmiF2aHIXw0MrFuvRDFX8Qvwci9cHiGm-asg1txvqXN5mBZQqpKpKWtBc9t29XoR916osQVoCqVuTJOe2YGoLmA/4ss/FlauVPTqTeGKDEKpox9WIg/h5/h001.DZgtd7-SMoK8qkJqGknsoiq4zcAhjixI1IpNwBVP7wc>
- the logical reading order of the change, not the order your platform
happens to sort it.

Every range gets its own plain-language summary, with sequence diagrams,
state machines, and ERDs generated inline wherever a visual earns its
place.

   -

   Cohorts group related files and chunks so you review one idea at a time.
   -

   Layers order them so foundational changes - data shapes, contracts -
   come before the code that depends on them.
   -

   Code Peek lets you click any variable, function, class or type to see
   its definition and usages without leaving the tab.
   -

   Semantic Diff cuts past formatting noise to show what actually changed.

The Best Prioritization Is No Prioritization
<https://link.mail.beehiiv.com/ss/c/u001.J3WaaJcD-G_P1A1xsf5CHvZqa6bLJQTQi_78LfVUM4p0iY-TbMOAKEU0YcY2hxtmpvacM27op-U5HwUCWYLh9cDCTtsUajEZWsG61uL5QYrFH9W2_s1OcPq2iMDjMQEJFwNxRVAIBeloQPGsz9MiKA0a2qVEGKMH_xIsgmeXTLLxkl04D_r8DpgOe-9ZRO6XUiGo5AJba9L71wPROMLwdQ/4ss/FlauVPTqTeGKDEKpox9WIg/h8/h001.XHL_xVMP7l6fUTXlzOYVtQiC4GU9TQLv3YIxENMUIqs>

*tl;dr*: “Prioritization is a trap. It wastes time, wastes effort, and
delivers worse results than just executing faster. Instead of spending your
time prioritizing: (1) Spend more time focusing on building faster. (2)
Shift everyone into durable teams that don’t have to do cross-business
prioritization at all, and manage your “prioritization” by the resourcing
decisions of growing, downsizing, or splitting teams.”
* Management Strategy*

You Don't Have To Be Smart If You Can Think Clearly

Review The Actual Change, Not The File List.
<https://link.mail.beehiiv.com/ss/c/u001.Ira8ofUIq_P_lCLURPEgQ8Dok6h2-kZwEtf4UdHH35jOOdsiawiN0pt6AAqOiyX86FZbHN7PqWqjfTjV0L0fhmQ2XcAQeg6QylDhfYMK2rlmmUn1PFN2-7tyH4X8V3fuO7ZSE5A20ZDG-xvX49KGqg/4ss/FlauVPTqTeGKDEKpox9WIg/h10/h001.1s4VXr_E2wYCtBPCb88Znh4x-9bU0ZmPWPLvDIuW0sc>

*tl;dr*: AI writes more code than ever. Reviewing it shouldn’t mean
scrolling forty files in alphabetical order. CodeRabbit Review reorganizes
any pull request from a flat file list into a structured, layer-by-layer
walkthrough - the logical reading order of the change, not the order your
platform happens to sort it. Every range gets its own plain-language
summary, with sequence diagrams, state machines, and ERDs generated inline
wherever a visual earns its place. (1) Cohorts group related files and
chunks so you review one idea at a time. (2) Layers order them so
foundational changes - data shapes, contracts - come before the code that
depends on them. (3) Code Peek lets you click any variable, function, class
or type to see its definition and usages without leaving the tab. (4)
Semantic Diff cuts past formatting noise to show what actually changed.
From the team that pioneered AI code reviews. 2M reviews every week. 6M
repos. 15K customers. Free during early access.

*Promoted by CodeRabbit*

— Dennis Brotzky

*tl;dr*: “A few milliseconds is all it takes to update an issue in Linear.
A traditional CRUD app doing the same thing takes about 300ms. How do they
do it? There's no secret silver bullet to performance. The reality is that
it's built from the ground up on the right foundation, then improved by
countless decisions. My goal is to walk through some of the techniques that
make Linear feel the way it does and help you implement the same.”

_aiste.ulozaite@gmail.com_

- Pointer's Issue #739 covers engineering management in the AI era, including how LLMs are upending 'old rules' of leadership and coping with change like desk moves.
- Featured deep dives explore agentic coding setups, testing AI agents at scale (Miro/Signadot case study), algebraic data types in software design, and cache-line-level performance optimization.
- Also includes career growth pieces on 'working loudly' in remote teams and the software developer as 'conductor,' plus a roundup of tool links (Hallmark, Mouzi, Stacked PRs, OpenWork) and a live session on measuring AI productivity.

— Rachel Laycock

*tl;dr*: “A great conductor is first and foremost a great musician. They
could play the instruments themselves. That’s not why they’re standing on
the podium. Their value comes from understanding the whole score. The
orchestra doesn’t need the conductor because the musicians aren’t talented
enough. It needs the conductor because someone has to hold the whole system
in their head. Increasingly, I think that’s what great software developers
are doing.”

<https://link.mail.beehiiv.com/ss/c/u001.3a5P_SwQzY5x8USD2q4p0m895QuVtqvkW11dSkgIg8I7yY6GUJWSXHFbDSHBQ6GNO97jmxS8vjQnwyyz4O2h9BR-DYNcxQc37-aKgDZlUstD0r3LTm4e0KFs3UMbZO0oT_ZRwffOYUv7GO1JJAoaJKP74yn-GMBaipXL1l8B_mP-trBWG_d-oD-jEMyyxo0sMmLOlJnZY45RskdLcKnO7WgTwO-MBlaHtWuWUfnpCVT-M-7JcY4akkqPGlK6et_YWNWZz0Jm_KQnMgaLX4OSCN1GyaCXBNsUPtZC-o0P1z-5wOtTKAJnj_RZ3DIAiVEg/4sw/eBYeDbHiRy6heWHd_5s8-A/h12/h001.b0CEs04LFaMF-iw8E9tza6cSOu3AQCS-JMplxXBqfEk>

*tl;dr*: Miro's AI team was building a new AI architecture across nearly
250 microservices, and one shared environment meant tests constantly
collided. With Signadot, they moved to verifying changes in parallel with
lightweight environments in the cluster. Agents drive full end-to-end tests
against live services and iterate before a PR opens. Engineers get verified
working code without waiting for slots.

different binary versions and dependency trees, and things break silently.
Flox lets you pin your entire AI coding environment, from Claude Code and
Copilot to CUDA dependencies. Your assistant knows exactly what's
installed, and every engineer runs the exact same setup.

### Fwd: LT#330 A small problem
_aiste.ulozaite@gmail.com_

- Lead essay uses Captain Eric Moody's 1982 'small problem' understatement to illustrate that communicating calm and matching messages to the audience is key leadership skill during crises
- Newsletter roundup covers workplace politics for engineers, 'zombie companies' that appear successful but have lost competitive edge, risks of offloading thinking to AI, and using 'Code Yellow' incident processes to fix chronic outages
- Industry links highlight Stripe/Advent's $53B PayPal buyout offer, circular GPU financing among Nvidia/CoreWeave/Nebius, Irish datacenter energy use, and security topics like threat modeling and social engineering risks

July 19, 2026 | Read Online
<https://leadershipintech.com/newsletters/2326?sid=027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4>

A small problem
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22776/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*8 minutes* by Mike Fisher

In 1982, Captain Eric Moody told passengers his 747 had "a small problem"
after all four engines failed over the Indian Ocean. That understatement
was deliberate: the cabin needed calm, while the cockpit got the full
brutal truth. The lesson is that composure is something you broadcast, not
just feel, and the message must match who receives it. Tell it to people
who cannot act and it steadies them. Tell it to people who must act and it
kills them.

AI Bots Are Changing How Enterprises Monitor and Manage Their Infrastructure
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22777/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*sponsored by Hydrolix*

AI bots are driving more requests, more telemetry, and new security
challenges across enterprise environments. But the biggest challenge isn't
detection. It's understanding what bots are doing, which ones can be
trusted, and how they affect performance, security, and customer
experience. Based on a survey of 300 enterprise IT and security leaders,
this report examines why organizations are investing in better visibility
and real-time analytics at scale to manage AI-driven traffic.

What does "playing politics" mean for software engineers?
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22778/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*13 minutes* by Sean Goedecke

Playing politics at work isn't about scheming. It means knowing who holds
real power, avoiding conflict with them, and actively helping them succeed.
When you disagree with a powerful person, do it privately, stay polite, and
drop it when overruled. Make sure your contributions are visible, and
respond quickly when senior people need help.

Zombie companies
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22779/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*44 minutes* by Jono Alderson

Large companies can keep growing, renewing contracts, and reporting strong
results long after their ability to build a relevant future has gone. Past
advantages like brand recognition, contracts, and switching costs keep
money flowing, but they say nothing about whether the business could earn
its position again today. Meanwhile, challengers need less capital than
ever to pick apart the most valuable pieces, and AI is making it cheaper
for customers to inspect, compare, and leave. The failure happens years
before the numbers show it.

Are we offloading too much of our thinking to AI?
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22780/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*8 minutes* by Yennie Jun

Offloading thinking to AI is increasingly common, from trivial daily
choices to complex reasoning. While this can free up time and boost
productivity, Yennie argues it risks eroding our autonomy and ability to
think independently. Using AI to check and extend our own thinking differs
from replacing that thinking entirely. The real question is whether we are
automating routine work or giving away our own judgment and desires.

Code yellow, code red
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22781/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*13 minutes* by James Stanier

James shares his experience of how repeated outages were eroding customer
trust, leading to a formal company-wide Code Yellow, targeting eight
consecutive weeks of zero downtime. The effort involved auditing the entire
stack, fixing rate limiting, adding circuit breakers, resolving memory
leaks, and overhauling incident processes. The target was hit. Code Yellows
work best with a clear problem statement, measurable exit criteria, full
organisational transparency, and genuine authority to reprioritise work
across teams.

### Fwd: LT#333 The best prioritization is no prioritization
_aiste.ulozaite@gmail.com_

- Prioritization frameworks often waste time; speed of shipping and consistent team resourcing beat elaborate decision-making processes.
- Featured reads cover the 'Antithesis Principle' of self-awareness, staff engineer problem-finding tactics, and why raw (unsmoothed) metrics reveal real business issues.
- A piece on LLMs argues domain expertise—like Terence Tao's math knowledge—is key to effectively steering AI models toward better outputs.

July 30, 2026 | Read Online
<https://leadershipintech.com/newsletters/2332?sid=027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4>

The best prioritization is no prioritization
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22841/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*6 minutes* by Stay Saasy

Prioritization frameworks waste time and rarely prove their worth. Instead,
build faster: speed beats clever decision-making every time, and shipping
more teaches you what works. Keep teams focused on one area permanently so
decisions stay simple and comparable. Treat resourcing as your main lever,
not constant replanning.

How much of your traffic is actually AI bots? Most teams are guessing low
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22842/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*sponsored by Hydrolix*

Industry benchmarks put automated traffic at over half of all web activity,
yet 82% of enterprises estimate AI bots account for under 25% of theirs.
That blind spot skews your analytics, degrades cache efficiency, and buries
real signals in telemetry noise. Based on a survey of 300 enterprise
leaders, this report breaks down how engineering teams are getting
real-time visibility into bot traffic at scale, without blocking the
automation they depend on.

The antithesis principle
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22843/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*5 minutes* by Shreyas Doshi

Most people notice how human tendencies like needing entertainment,
following charisma, or judging first impressions affect others. The smarter
move is also turning that observation inward and working to eliminate those
same tendencies in yourself. This is the Antithesis Principle: truths about
human nature point both outward as tactics and inward as warnings. Smart
people find the tactic. Wise people also find the warning.

How I find problems to solve as a staff engineer
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22844/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*10 minutes* by Lalit Maganti

Finding good problems to work on comes from paying close attention to what
people struggle with day to day, not from scheduled thinking sessions.
Letting issues accumulate over time reveals patterns: separate complaints
often share a common root cause. Testing ideas through prototypes and
conversations helps confirm whether a solution is real or just elegant on
paper. Over time, solving the right problems builds trust and opens doors
to shaping what a team builds next.

Please don't smooth the metrics
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22845/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*5 minutes* by Dave Kellogg

Smoothing metrics helps investors spot long-term trends, but operators
should avoid it. It hides the very spikes and dips that signal real
problems. A worked example shows how smoothed churn masked a sales team
expanding to the wrong customers, delaying a critical board conversation by
six months while the damage grew. Raw, volatile numbers tell the truth.

LLMs reward expertise
<https://leadershipintech.com/links/22846/027d9985-d4c5-48a7-af59-5152efd9c8a4/email>
*4 minutes* by Sean Goedecke

Domain knowledge is the most important skill when working with LLMs.
Terence Tao's ChatGPT conversation about advanced mathematics shows this
clearly. He could steer the model, spot weak answers, and suggest better
directions because he already understood the subject deeply. The same
applies to coding or any other field: the more you know, the more you can
push the model toward what you actually want.

Small programming tricks
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22716/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*4 minutes* by Will Keleher

Small tricks and shortcuts add up to meaningful gains in engineering
productivity. Things like fuzzy shell history search, git pickaxe, or
knowing a single command to spin up a local server can save real time
without requiring deep background knowledge. The same applies inside
companies, where knowing who to ask or where to find a doc can be just as
valuable. Sharing one tip a day with your team is a simple way to spread
this kind of knowledge.

Share your AI agent's work with your team
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22717/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*sponsored by Display*

Your agent generates a report, dashboard, or prototype as HTML or Markdown.
display.dev
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22718/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
turns it into a secure URL your team can open with their existing work
login, comment on inline, and send back to any agent through MCP. Works
with Cursor, Codex, Claude Code, and more. No viewer accounts, no seat
management, no vendor lock-in. Learn more.

When ‘if’ slows you down, avoid it
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22719/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*4 minutes* by Christof Kaser

Branch mispredictions slow CPUs down because they force the processor to
discard queued work and start over. Writing branchless code removes
unpredictable jumps and lets the CPU run at full speed. A simple filter
loop rewritten to use a conditional increment instead of an if statement
ran nearly 10 times faster on an Apple M1. Compilers cannot make this
change automatically because they cannot safely assume unconditional memory
writes are always allowed.

Running an obfuscated bash script from Uniqlo's tee
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22720/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*6 minutes* by Tristan Sherliker

A Uniqlo t-shirt made in partnership with Akamai contains a real bash
script printed on the back, encoded in base64. After carefully transcribing
and decoding it, the script turns out to be a harmless Easter egg that
displays a colorful animated "Peace for All" message in the terminal.
Akamai designed the shirt as a nod to the early internet era, with the code
serving as a reference to Linux.

Virtual memory from first principles
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22721/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*about 1 hour* by Abhinav Upadhyay

Abhinav provides a practical introduction to virtual memory, explaining its
core mechanisms, including address translation, demand paging,
copy-on-write, memory mapping, and page reclaim. He also explores their
impact on system performance, memory management, and observability,
equipping readers with a mental model for building and debugging
data-intensive systems.

Advice to new grads: Take any job you can get in the industry
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22722/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*4 minutes* by Jim Grey

Getting into the software industry matters more than getting the perfect
role right away. Taking jobs in support, QA, or technical writing puts you
close to real problems and real people. Relationships built through good
work open doors that portfolios alone cannot. Proximity to the right people
and problems is what moves careers forward.

July 26, 2026 | Read Online
<https://programmingdigest.net/newsletters/2328?sid=91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47>

The depth-first search pattern: Exploring trees and graphs
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22798/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*15 minutes* by Franco Fernando

Depth-first search is a recursion pattern for exploring trees, graphs, and
matrices. It works by going as deep as possible along one path before
backtracking and trying another. To avoid infinite loops in graphs, you
track visited nodes and skip them on future visits. Depth-first search fits
problems where you need to find paths, check reachability, or explore all
possibilities through a structure.

Stop re-explaining your codebase to your AI agent every morning
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22799/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*sponsored by Jolli*

Coding agents are stateless, your context isn't. Jolli Memory stores agent
memory as Git-backed files in your repo, so it persists across sessions and
follows you between Cursor, Claude Code, Codex, and VS Code. Ships with the
codebase to every teammate. Free and open source.

Everyone should know SIMD
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22800/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*11 minutes* by Mitchell Hashimoto

SIMD lets a CPU process multiple values at once instead of one at a time,
turning slow byte-by-byte loops into chunk-based operations that run 4x to
16x faster. Despite its reputation for complexity, common SIMD code follows
the same five steps every time: broadcast constants, loop over chunks,
operate on all values at once, reduce the result, then handle leftovers
with a normal loop. Once you learn that pattern, writing SIMD is nearly as
simple as writing a regular loop.

htop explained
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22801/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*43 minutes* by Pēteris Ņikiforovs

Linux tools like htop and top expose a lot of system data, and Pēteris
walks through what each number and column actually means. Load average is
not simply CPU usage — it counts all running and waiting processes,
including those stuck on disk or network. Memory columns like VIRT and RES
can be misleading, since virtual memory includes mapped but unused space.
Pēteris also covers process states, signals, niceness, scheduling, and what
each background service on a fresh Linux server actually does.

In-house LLM serving at Netflix
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22802/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*12 minutes* by Netflix Technology Blog

Netflix runs its own LLM serving stack on top of vLLM and NVIDIA Triton,
integrated into existing production infrastructure rather than a separate
system. Key decisions around engine choice, model packaging, API design,
and deployment strategies each revealed unexpected trade-offs only under
real production load. A notable example is constrained decoding, where
per-request CPU processing caused latency to grow linearly with batch size
until a rewrite using vLLM V1's batch-level API and C++ solved the
bottleneck.

I inspected my take-home interview project. It was a whole operation.
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22803/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*9 minutes* by Appaji C

A fake recruiter on LinkedIn sent Appaji a Python developer job offer with
a take-home coding test hidden inside a zip file. The project looked clean
at first, but running a simple directory listing revealed pre-loaded Git
hooks designed to silently download and execute malicious scripts the
moment any Git command ran. The scripts then installed Node.js in the
background and ran obfuscated code targeting crypto wallets, with unique
tracking IDs assigned to each victim.

August 02, 2026 | Read Online

How big is a Git commit?
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22855/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*5 minutes* by Dave Gauer

Git stores data as compressed objects, so commit sizes vary based on how
compressible your files are. Small commits add a few kilobytes of overhead,
while larger text files can compress down to around 10% of their original
size. Git also packs objects together over time to save even more space,
making it very storage efficient overall.

Stop re-explaining your codebase to your AI agent every morning
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22856/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*sponsored by Jolli*

Coding agents are stateless, your context isn't. Jolli Memory stores agent
memory as Git-backed files in your repo, so it persists across sessions and
follows you between Cursor, Claude Code, Codex, and VS Code. Ships with the
codebase to every teammate. Free and open source.

Harness engineering deep dive
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22857/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*30 minutes* by Lukas Niessen

The model is just a small part of a real AI agent system. The harness — the
loop, tools, sandbox, state, sensors, and guardrails — is where most of the
engineering actually lives. Sensors should transform tool output into clean
signals; the agent should never decide it is done on its own; and action
spaces shrink through tool design, not longer instructions. The outer
harness is the durable asset that compounds over time, while models get
swapped and commoditized.

How LLMs figure out what you mean
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22858/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*6 minutes* by Zarar Siddiqi

An AI can't work with raw text, so it converts each word into a list of
numbers called a vector. Think of each vector as an arrow, where words with
similar meanings point in similar directions. The model then scores how
closely any two arrows align and converts those scores into percentage
weights, showing how much attention each word pays to every other word.

Meet alice. Alice is impatient.
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22859/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*4 minutes* by Marc Brooker

Users and operators often measure service performance very differently.
Operators count each outage or slow request once, while users experience
long outages repeatedly, making them feel much worse. This is called the
inspection paradox: users see a time-weighted version of your metrics, so a
one-minute average recovery time can feel like an hour to them. Long tail
events matter far more to users than standard averages suggest.

A peek into reddit's anti-spam internals
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22860/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*24 minutes* by Lyra Horse

A bug in Reddit's spam filter briefly exposed internal removal reasons to a
moderator in 2021. The leaked messages revealed several tools Reddit uses
behind the scenes, including Google's Perspective API for spam scoring, a
rules engine called Spamurai, domain bans, and even URL inspection that
scans linked pages for known spam patterns.

And the most popular article from the last issue was:

   - Stop re-explaining your codebase to your AI agent every morning
   <https://programmingdigest.net/links/22861/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>

_aiste.ulozaite@gmail.com_

- Lead article explores how time (wall-clock, monotonic, logical, business) is a tricky design boundary in production systems, covering clock drift, scheduling, time zones, and reconciliation
- Other featured reads include building a decentralized messenger protocol via federation, handling production incidents calmly, and growing engineering skills without over-relying on AI
- Sponsored piece from LangChain covers infrastructure for reliable long-running AI agents; a stats article explains Gosset's t-distribution correction for small sample confidence intervals

August 09, 2026 | Read Online
<https://programmingdigest.net/newsletters/2339?sid=91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47>

Beyond happy path engineering: Time
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22959/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*37 minutes* by Gabor Koos

Gabor explains why time is more complicated in production systems than it
first appears. He distinguishes wall-clock, monotonic, logical, and
business time, showing when each should be used. He also covers clock
drift, expiration, scheduled jobs, time zones, competing updates, and
reconciliation. The main lesson is to treat time as a design boundary and
make its assumptions explicit.

Ship long-horizon agents that hold up in production
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22960/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*sponsored by LangChain*

Long-running agents break the assumptions behind standard app
infrastructure. They can run for minutes or hours, pause for human input,
recover from worker crashes, and still need controls around cost, memory,
code execution, and tracing. Download the engineering guide to the runtime
primitives that keep production agents reliable.

How to create your own decentralized messenger protocol
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22961/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*15 minutes* by Michał Karbowiak

Michał guides on building a simple decentralized messaging protocol using
federation. He covers server discovery, user identities, end-to-end
encryption, public key exchange, message delivery, and server
authentication. He shows how clients can encrypt messages while servers
securely communicate and verify each other. The approach provides a
practical foundation for building a simple, decentralized messenger without
relying on a central server.

Notes on incidents
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22962/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*6 minutes* by Sean Goedecke

Most incidents resolve without any action, and rushing to fix things often
makes them worse. The best first step is to do nothing, take a breath, and
observe. Simple fixes like disabling a feature flag usually solve the
problem, but only if you know the system well enough to act decisively.
Fixing incidents earns goodwill, but it is not a reliable path to lasting
influence.

Growing as an engineer in a world of AI
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22963/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*18 minutes* by Norberto Lopes

Norberto argues that leaning on AI to generate code without understanding
it stunts your growth as an engineer. The learning that used to happen
through struggle, repetition, and debugging is exactly what AI removes. To
grow, he suggests treating AI as a thinking partner rather than an answer
machine: ask why, type code out instead of pasting it, read the
foundational books, and stay close to the hard conversations AI cannot join.

90% of the t distribution
<https://programmingdigest.net/links/22964/91ee7b33-c28b-4a8e-bab4-e5b8bc4f3f47/email>
*3 minutes* by Kqr

Gosset found that using a normal distribution to calculate confidence
intervals gives results that are too narrow, because it treats an estimated
standard deviation as exact. He developed correction factors based on
sample size to fix this. With just two data points, you can estimate a
standard deviation by multiplying the difference between them by 1.3, which
helps judge whether a result is truly unusual.
